ParlaMint-NL_2017-11-09-tweedekamer-16.u21	Mr. Chairman. This cabinet is going to reform the tax system. This tax reform is based on three pillars. One is the income package, two is the business package and three is the environmental package. I'll make a brief introduction here. For the more detailed explanation of the tax measures, please refer to the Secretary of State. Mr. Chairman. I'll start with the income package, including purchasing power. The first pillar of the tax reform is the income package. The cabinet chooses to allow citizens to benefit more from the favourable economic dynamics. The burden on citizens will be reduced, inter alia by introducing the two-wheel system and increasing tax reductions in income tax. All this is accompanied by a cost reduction of 6.2 billion in 2021. Of these, 5.2 billion are made up of lower taxes and 1 billion are spent. These are mainly increased supplements, such as the childcare allowance and the child-related budget. I would like to repeat once again that this will improve the purchasing power of all the income groups in the coming years. In addition, the package presented by this cabinet also reduces income inequality. I would also like to emphasise this, because that was one of the things that was clearly reflected in the Plan Bureau's report. I imagine many in this house embrace that, too. In addition to the reduction of the burden, the income package includes reforms. This will increase the burden on consumption in the future. Also, the maximum rate of deduction for deductions under which the mortgage interest rate deduction is to be deducted. We already exchanged a few thoughts about this in four steps to the base rate. The proceeds of the acceleration of the write-off of the mortgage interest rate deduction will then be used again. It'll be lowered over a million dollars. I have tried to summarize the Dutch story for the foreign colleagues in three c's. The first c is that of "commitment." The second c is that of "groups." The third c is that of "conditions." By commitment, I mean commitment, and I also meant to say to colleagues that the Netherlands was committed, is and remains the responsibility of Europe and the euro area. That is why I have also said that it is really crucial for a well-functioning EMU that those conditions are met by everyone. The c of "conditions" is actually the resultant of those first two c's, you might say, because we have that commitment, but we also have great concerns. We think it is important that these concerns are addressed and that we have the house in order in Europe before we talk about other things, such as fiscal capacity. As you know, the coalition agreement is also very specific and restricts it. A specific issue that has emerged, let me mention it right away, is the banking union. As you know, the Netherlands is fully in favour of this, but there is a very important point of attention, and that is the deposit guarantee scheme. It is crucial that I have tried to explain with the severity that some people here do not like, that it cannot be that the debts are mutualised before they are reduced. The Stability and Growth Pact is truly the cornerstone of European society. As I said to colleagues in the House, and also in one-on-one conversations, I say here too: it is worrying when you look at that picture in a period of economic growth and see that a lot of countries are on the wrong side of the line of that 3% and certainly of that 60%. We all know the proverb "never washed a good crisis." I think the opposite is also true. You must take advantage of the economic situation that is now experienced in Europe, and fortunately, also to reduce those debts further. That is the most important and first appropriate way of achieving what we call fiscal capacity in Brussels. That is what I have fully and very explicitly shared. He was critical of the appointment of the President of the ECB as permanent chairman of that European Systemic Risk Board and wanted to know if we can do anything about this. This is also mentioned in the BNC-fiche which has previously ended up in the Chamber. Why do we agree with Mr Omtzigt? That's actually very simple. The ECB President is also responsible for monetary policy. That is why the cabinet, like Mr Omtzigt, and I suspect everyone in the Chamber, would have liked us to have separated it. We will also continue to work for a different solution. The next question about the ECB was about the buy-back programme. Mr Omtzigt wanted to know what the financial risk is for the Netherlands; how we can control and reduce it; and whether I wanted to enthuse the Court of Auditors to move. The first part of the answer is always "recognized" by the Chamber, but I am going to say again that as Minister of Finance I have to and want to acknowledge that the ECB is independent in its policy. This independence is sometimes difficult, but it is important and we should really leave it that way as far as I am concerned. At the same time, we are aware of the risks. This reduction covers, for the whole amount, compensation for the accelerated phasing-out of the mortgage interest rate deduction. Moreover, the reduction of the housing benefit results in the effect of phasing out the Hills scheme being mitigated for the group which has no or little property debt. Mr Van Rooijen asked whether the cabinet would not reconsider the proposal on the Hills Scheme or whether an exception could be made for existing cases in the phasing-out of Hillen, so that they would not be unduly confronted with this discharge in his words. The repayment requirement for the property debt introduced in 2013 was an additional incentive for accelerated repayment, reducing the need for the Hillen Act. Moreover, the Hills scheme is financially unsustainable in the long term, as stated in the coalition agreement. We're talking cost over a billion dollars. As a result of the repayment requirement, taxpayers are much more likely to benefit from the Hillen scheme.

That does not allow a really good comparison of this example. Then the request was whether I could look at all the rules around the mortgages. In any case, I would like to say that the introduction of compulsory annuity relief has already resulted in a huge simplification compared to the rather complex mortgage products of the past. For new mortgages, annuity redemption has become the norm. The mortgage lending standards are generally called the Nibud standards "Nibud" as simple as possible. They must avoid over-indebtedness on the part of consumers. We think that's important. Moreover, where it is justified, the sector may deviate from the scheme. So I'm not saying "where it's possible" but "where it's justified." Basically, that's the line. In addition, the Minister for Home Affairs and Kingdom Relations, together with the Ministry of Finance and the Sector, has set up the Platform Customisation, which examines, on the basis of market practice, whether the regulation is sufficiently in line with what is offered in the market. I think that has been an important new development. Mr Baudet asked if the cabinet could support the motion to break open the combined Tax Plan. I didn't know exactly which block to put this question in, so I'm kind of treating it right now at the end of the first block. The cabinet aims to achieve the widest possible distribution of the filing of tax legislation to be dealt with, so that not only the legislator but also the Chamber has sufficient time to do so. In my opinion, draft collective legislation such as the Tax Plan remains necessary and useful for measures such as substantive and budgetary coherence. I can also note that the cabinet commits itself to a note on collective legislation on this type of bill, which is laid down in Parliament's 32500-VI, letter M, from the 2010-2011 meeting year, as stated in the footnote. We therefore have certain rules on whether or not to deal with matters in collective legislation. For example, within a collective law, there must be a clearly defined concept of substantive or budgetary coherence. This applies, for example, to legislation which uses the proceeds of certain measures as cover for other measures. Logically, you want it in one bill. Collecting laws are also possible if there is a thematic coherence, for example if you are proposing a number of tax evasion laws, to name but a few, or if there is a technical coherence of implementation. This can also be done: measures can have the same kind of effect or an opposite effect on implementation. You want to look at them in conjunction, so that all implementing authorities can implement the law equally. This policy has also been promoted since 2016, including by the Council of State and also by previous parliamentary questions and motions on this subject. We've always kept to that.

That's different from the theory that sometimes it's not crazy to throw some parts into one law. I explained that here. We shouldn't just do that. We understand that this can lead to problems, all these things together. We must take a decision on this. Sometimes it is wise to do this together, for example in the case of a tax plan, because it has a strong budgetary coherence, including purchasing power and burden.

Again, this is what we are doing with withholding tax in 2020 on dividends in case of abuses and outgoing flows to countries with very low taxes, the low-tax jurisdictions mentioned by Mrs Leijten as tax havens. In short, the Netherlands will no longer be sought as a transit country in this kind of tariffs, and that is what we are all looking for, I think. We consider the international approach to tax avoidance supported by the Netherlands to be very important. We want to play an active part in this, as I just said. Tax avoidance is by nature an international problem because the differences between tax systems are abused. An international problem also requires an international solution. The previous cabinet has already taken steps in the context of the OECD and the EU. The Paradise Papers once again show that tax avoidance must and will have our continued attention. The cabinet will not only act on this internationally, but also intends to take national steps, as I have just explained. The coalition agreement contains a number of these measures. The cabinet will pay attention to the locational environment for companies that want to develop in the Netherlands. We'll keep an eye on this. That does not mean, by the way, I believe that Mr Snels found that the Netherlands is a facilitator, as it was called, of tax avoidance in these new plans. On the contrary, I think. By introducing withholding taxes on both interest, royalties and dividends, we are stopping this. Mr Snels also asked why, if he is serious about fighting tax avoidance earlier, the CV/e.g. structure in the Netherlands, everyone in the Netherlands seems to know what that is before the end of 2020. In itself, I get that. We note that this problem is now there. Then why does it take a while before it is really abolished, on 1 January 2020? This too has to do with the technique and complexity of such a legislative proposal. We have decided to consult on this first. That is also the wish of the Chamber. We are currently preparing legislation that we are going to present early next year for the internet consultation. Then the cabinet can start implementing the ATAD 2 legislative project in the course of 2018, after that has been done. Then it goes to the Council of State and then it comes into your Chamber. This should be dealt with in 2018, 2019. If we can agree on this in 2019, I hope that, of course, 1 January is a known, fixed date of entry into force. I think that 1 January 2020 is really the earliest possible date if we want to do this seriously and if we want to go through all these processes with the corresponding steps. It is technically difficult, but there is also another argument. You also have to look at the implementation practice if you were to put this even further forward. The performers must also be ready. They need to understand the new law. I have already mentioned it in another legislative proposal: I really want to take that into account when we change things here that have a lot of impact. That is why we are coming to 2020. They also asked: can we stop issuing rulings? I believe that is what Mrs Leijten asked for. I do not think that is desirable for a number of reasons. First of all, it is not the case that a ruling is anything other than the outcome of a legal process, within all case law and so on. The only thing that happens in a ruling is that you get the certainty that you hope to get afterwards from every tax inspector around the world and that a Dutch tax inspector says: if this is the fact complex, we can do so in advance. We think that's a great thing. We consider the rulings, giving certainty to entrepreneurs, important. We want to continue with this. Stopping it is undesirable. Moreover, stopping has an additional disadvantage. In these rulings, as I have already pointed out, there is a lot of information contained in the case of the company that is working on a structure. We are now handing these rulings over to other EU countries. They'll have the details of how we handle it. That makes it easier for them to see if there are any tax obligations or rights with them anywhere. In the legislative consultation it has been mentioned once or twice that the rulings also provide a lot of information, not only for the Tax Administration, but also for the countries with which we exchange them. In the context of the Paradise Papers, the ruling practice has once again fallen under a magnifying glass. Mrs Leijten and Mr Snels have asked who is going to conduct the review of the rulings and when is reported on them. I have given orders to check the rulings with cross-border effect. We came out with that yesterday morning in the letter to your room. It is going to be carefully checked whether all these rulings meet all the standards we have for that. We are not going to let the butcher judge his own meat, so we are not going to let the people responsible for that at the Treasury, but we are going to have people look at it who understand process and procedures. I will report to the Chamber at the beginning of next year on the results of that investigation. Mr. Chairman. With that, I think we have had most of the questions in that area. I'm sorry, I have another one. Mr Van Weyenberg asked when the Chamber could receive the business case announced in the government agreement on improving the information position and the tax authorities' detection capacity. I have a map of where and how this reinforcement can best be designed. I would, however, like to come up with a well-founded plan, both in terms of the possibilities for improvement and in terms of information position and detection capacity. It must also be clear what it can bring. I would, of course, also like to identify the costs involved. I do not want to commit to a deadline now, because I think care is a little more important than speed. But I do understand the idea behind this. For me too, it is a high priority to combat tax avoidance, in this case tax evasion. I really want to speed up, but we shouldn't say "then and then we'll have that done," because before you know it, we have a deadline that we can't meet. Speed is very important, but I don't have a date yet. Mr Bruins I do not know if he is still present; yes, there he is! I was asked whether the treaties with the non-cooperative jurisdictions would be reviewed. At European level, the EU list of non-cooperative jurisdictions is currently under hard work. General measures against these jurisdictions are also discussed. It is sensible if not every country that does it for itself, but the EU really is a front in this. That's actually going pretty well. In the past, that was complicated, but we're well on our way. Moreover, no agreement has yet been reached on this, but we intend to establish that EU list at the Ecofin Council on 5 December. Then it becomes very clear who is and who is not on that list. But to respond to that now, before this point is negotiated and final, I think it is a little early. You'll have the answer within a month. I think I have answered the questions on this point.

ParlaMint-NL_2017-11-09-tweedekamer-16.u437	Yeah, the third block is greening. This cabinet is going to green the Netherlands, just to use a central sentence. So we are going to ensure that the climate objectives are met. This includes further greening of the tax system. The 'polluter pays' principle is very important to us. A better pricing of external environmental costs increases the incentive to reduce pollution. That's a classic greening idea. We really support that. The pricing of CO emissions will soon contribute to the objectives set by this cabinet, which are also mentioned in the Paris Climate Treaty. At the same time, higher tax revenues are used to reduce other taxes, as indicated above. The greening of the tax system is therefore beneficial for both employment and the environment. We really believe that greening is not for one thing or another, but that greening can help both employment and the environment. For greening in general, the emphasis is of course not only on pollution and non-pricing. Greening is also the move from direct to indirect taxes. You might ask yourself, is this all the cabinet does? No, but it's definitely an element that you have to include in your arrangements. Direct taxes are more disruptive. If you join indirect taxes such as consumption, you are closer to the source of pollution and overconsumption than when you charge wages, so to speak. I deal with a number of specific measures. The national CO minimum price is important. With this measure, CO emissions in the Netherlands are priced in addition to the ETS. The cabinet also balances energy taxes better, in relation to CO emissions, and reduces the burden reduction in this tax base. From 2020 onwards, the balancing scheme in the energy tax will be replaced by a more cost-effective subsidy scheme, which we believe will really contribute to achieving the objectives. The Cabinet also increases the tax rate for the incineration and landfill of waste from 2019. In particular, this measure should contribute to the transition to a more circular economy. The cabinet also improves the pricing of environmental pollution by tackling flying. First of all, of course, we want to do so in a European context. Logically, if we introduce a measure somewhere, we do not want to fly in Belgium, Germany or another border area. That doesn't help the environment. The ideal situation is, of course, that you do that with each other. By the way, a number of countries already have a flight tax, so we do not have to start completely from scratch. If it turns out that it really isn't possible, then we're just gonna start ourselves; we've built that in here a little bit. Then we know that this is not as efficient as when we do it at EU level, but we really want to examine whether a national tax on noisy or at least polluting aircraft might be possible. The cabinet also abolishes the bpm refund scheme for taxis. This may not be the greatest measure, but it does provide a strong price for CO emissions from taxis. Finally, the cabinet also reduces the landlord levy for landlords who invest in the sustainability of their housing stock. 100 million has been earmarked for this purpose. Then there are a number of questions: there are really only a few questions that have been put on this subject in the first term. This includes storage for renewable energy and including VAT. We do not recognise ourselves in a €220 higher energy bill for households. We assume €140. That is still a significant increase, but a lot less than €220. Of course, this burden increase is not an element that you have to look at in isolation, but it is part of the coalition agreement. It also includes other measures to reduce the burden on these people. Here again, therefore, the overall package is important for purchasing power development throughout the cabinet period. Mr President, in the coalition agreement, the coalition parties made agreements that have an effect on the locational climate, and specifically on the tax location climate. And it's, I think, good to remind you of the purpose of that. The aim is that the Netherlands is and remains a country that is attractive for companies to settle in. That is the goal, for the simple reason that it creates jobs. In this way, Dutch companies can also continue to trade around the world. The Cabinet implements the measures from ATAD 1 and ATAD 2, and introduces a withholding tax on dividends, interest and royalties on outgoing flows. The cabinet also advocates the creation of a blacklist of tax havens. I think we have the connection with what was said before the break. The second part is the broadening of the basis. The base in the Vpb is widened, for example by limiting the interest deduction, increasing the rate in the Innovation Box and limiting the loss settlement. These are all subjects which, I think, have also been exchanged with the Secretary of State and may still be exchanged. The third piece is the relief. The VPB rates go to 16% and to 21%. This total package from the coalition agreement is good for the tax settlement environment. It strengthens the competitiveness of the Netherlands vis-à-vis the neighbouring countries. ParlaMint-NL_2017-11-09-tweedekamer-16.u611	Thank you. In itself, I don't think I have much to add to a lot of what has just been discussed here. Mr Dijkgraaf had a question that in itself is very relevant in its entirety: if you take measures that do not affect anyone at all, what do you do? Is there a channel to think it does happen? The question is, of course, quite correct and, once again, with all the answers or measures or thoughts I put forward here, it is never the killer's argument to prove: this was the proof and therefore it has been decided whether or not to do so. No one can do that, I am sure, not the supporters, not the opponents. There is no one who can give the killing argument, but a few facts may not be wrong, but I agree with that. Mr Dijkgraaf asked whether there were sufficient shareholders for which the dividend tax is not actually a withholding tax, but a final charge. Otherwise, the whole argument about the shareholdership is not worth much either. From a Dutch perspective, the dividend tax that affects the dividend of a foreign portfolio holder is always a final charge. We'll start with that. Then the question, according to my interpretation: is it conceivable that Dutch dividend tax could not be offset in the country of residence or residence of a foreign portfolio shareholder? The answer to that is yes. You can think of all kinds of examples. I shall mention a few. Think of individuals who, as a result of local tax rules in their state of residence, do not have the possibility to compensate. Those are quite a few, and not entirely coincidental, I say, are also examples of this in the UK. In the UK, no set-off is possible. Small investors benefit from an exemption on the dividend received up to £5,000; above that they are often taxed at reduced rates. Consequently, the Dutch dividend tax cannot or cannot be fully offset. What may also be the case, and of which we should not underestimate the impact, is that investment funds, which are often and in many countries exempted from tax on profits, cannot be compensated either. The result may be that individuals who are once again small investors, it is not just the large shareholders who have to invest through a foreign investment club or an investment fund and see dividend tax, must remain in an investment fund. Furthermore, it may also be the case with foreign pension funds. You should be aware that pension funds are not small investors worldwide. This applies not only to Dutch pension funds, but also to pension funds from many other countries, for example English and Canadian or American pension funds. Or how about charitative settings? This also applies in most countries, as in the Netherlands, to a different tax regime and in this way they cannot be compensated for the dividend tax. Let me give you just a few examples. The fact that they are exempt may have to do with a social function of such institutions in the country or with local regulations. The bottom line is that for these people the dividend tax is simply hanging on. He's gonna come in as a tax.
